251. **"The control was added" is not "the control is visible" — a test that asserts presence, size and position still passes for something that paints nothing, and a code path whose ONLY trigger is a `DispatcherTimer` cannot be tested headlessly at all.** Two testing lessons from the same investigation, both about tests that were green while the feature was broken. (a) The Q3 bulb tests asserted it was added to the overlay, measured non-zero and positioned inside the view — none asserted it had anything to paint **with** (gotcha #250). The missing assertions are cheap and decisive: for an icon, `Data` **and** `Foreground`/`Fill` non-null, plus `Opacity > 0`; asserting `IsEffectivelyVisible` is *not* enough, since it was `true` throughout. (b) Headless Avalonia runs **no `DispatcherTimer`** under `Dispatcher.UIThread.RunJobs()` + `Thread.Sleep` pumping — measured directly with a control timer (0 ticks in 2 s). So while the bulb's show path depended on a 450 ms dwell, the one link that mattered was the one link no test could reach; the fix was to **remove the timer dependency** (evaluate on the caret event, which is safe when the indicator is anchored to a span and does nothing when that span is unchanged), which made the failing path testable in the same move. **Rule: if a behaviour's only trigger is a timer, either drive it from an event instead or accept that it is live-QA-only — and say so in the test file rather than writing an assertion that passes for unrelated reasons.** *(Stage Q / Q3, 2026-07-25; `docs/history/20-...`)*

252. **An editor popup is keyboard-complete on day one — preselection, ↑/↓, Enter, Esc, and a SINGLE click equal to Enter — and that is part of the editor's contract, not later UI polish.** Any menu or list that participates in writing code (completion, Code Actions, future Refactorings/Generate/Surround) must ship with the whole model: the first item armed as it opens, arrows moving the selection, Enter (and Tab where it does not collide) running the armed one, Escape closing without acting, and one mouse click doing exactly what Enter does. Two implementation notes make it real rather than nominal. **Focus must not be the mechanism:** an `OverlayLayer`-hosted `ListBox` does not reliably take keyboard focus, so the popup keeps focus in the editor and handles the keys itself — and it must do so on the **TUNNEL** route, because AvaloniaEdit's `TextArea` handles Escape and the arrows at the source and a bubble handler never sees them (the same trap as #224 for Tab). **And a single click is not a second behaviour:** the `ListBox` has already moved the selection onto the pressed item by the time `PointerReleased` fires, so routing that straight to "apply the selection" makes click and Enter literally one operation instead of two to keep in step (a `DoubleTapped` handler is the wrong gesture — it makes the user click twice for one decision). The Q3/Q5 menu shipped mouse-only and needed a QA round to grow the keyboard; designing the next popup to this contract from the start is cheaper than retro-fitting it. *(Stage Q / Q5, 2026-07-25; `NavigationController.OnTunnelKey`; `docs/history/20-...`)*

253. **Tearing down an async resource while a background operation is still using it does not just risk a race — it manufactures a FALSE error, because the operation throws on return and the generic `catch` reports it as a failure of the work rather than of the teardown.** The debugger's "an edit ends the session" rule had to end a session that might be mid-step: stepping runs on `Task.Run` (the executor is sync-over-async), and `RunStepAsync`'s `catch` maps any exception to `Phase = Faulted` + a red Error Bar. Disposing the session's attachment from the edit handler would therefore have shown the user a fabricated Firebird fault for something they did deliberately — the teardown's own exception wearing the failed operation's clothes. **The fix is not a lock and not a cancellation token: it is to let the in-flight operation finish and end the resource in its own tail.** A flag set by the requester (`_condemnedByEdit`) is consumed at the two places the wire operation returns — the success path *and* the `catch`, which must check it FIRST so a condemned operation's exception is read as the teardown it is. **Rule: when an event handler wants to dispose something a background task is holding, hand the request to that task's completion path instead of disposing under it — and make every `catch` in that path ask "was this failure requested?" before it reports one.** (Applies to any VM that disposes a connection/session/reader outside the async method that uses it.) *(Debugger "edit ends the session", 2026-07-25; `DebuggerTabViewModel.OnSourceChanged` / `EndSessionForEditAsync`; `docs/history/19-...`)*

254. **A “before” value must be REMEMBERED when it was true, never re-derived at comparison time from state that has since started following the user's input — or the check quietly compares the new value with itself and always passes.** The debugger asks “is the launch panel still describing this routine?” by comparing a launch signature (object kind + ordered input parameters, or a trigger's header + referenced NEW/OLD columns) before and after. That was written as `var before = BuildLaunchSignature();` at the top of the save — correct only while the parsed model described the DATABASE text. The moment the model was made to follow the edit buffer, any re-parse between the two readings (a gutter click is enough) made `before` and `after` the same reading, so a genuinely changed parameter list would have been declared “still valid” and the session relaunched against a panel that no longer matched. The fix is to store the signature **at the moment the panel is built** (`_panelSignature`) — the thing the question is actually about. **Rule: when a comparison guards “has what the user already decided gone stale?”, capture the value where the decision was made; a re-derivation is safe only while its source of truth cannot move under it, and “cannot move” stops being true the moment a feature makes that source live.** *(Debugger Seam B — Restart runs the draft, 2026-07-25; `DebuggerTabViewModel._panelSignature`; `docs/history/19-...`)*

256. **Prove first, mutate second — a method that starts changing the model and only then discovers the value is unusable leaves a state nobody chose, and it looks exactly like a value the user entered.** `ExecuteProcedureParamRowViewModel.ApplyHistoryValue` restored a stored parameter value by setting `IsNull = false` and *then* trying to parse the text into the typed holder. On a parse failure it simply skipped the assignment — so the row was left un-NULLed with the **constructor's default** showing (`0` for a numeric), indistinguishable on screen from something somebody had typed, and it would have been submitted to the server as one. The fix is ordering, not extra validation: parse into a local, and touch the row only once the value exists. This generalises well past parsing — **any apply / restore / import path should compute the whole new state before writing the first field**, because a half-applied model is a state no code intended and no user chose. It is the discipline of a transaction, at the object level. It matters most exactly where a project has just adopted a "never guess" rule, because a partially-applied value **is** a guess, made by accident. The shape to watch for is `flag = X; if (TryParse(…)) field = Y;` — the flag is already committed to a value the parse may never produce. *(Debugger C3.2, 2026-07-25; `docs/history/19-...`)*

257. **A persisted value cannot be re-applied safely unless it also records the TYPE it was entered under — and when you add such a field, the two places that silently drop it are the store's hand-written re-projection and its value-equality helper.** EmberTern's parameter history stored `{ Name, IsNull, Text }`: enough to show a value, not enough to know whether it still means anything. After a parameter went `INTEGER → VARCHAR` the restore matched by name and put `"10"` into a text field — the exact IBExpert behaviour the debugger's carry-over rule was written to reject, sitting in our own code. Recording the declared type turns the restore into a **proof** (re-apply only when the stored type classifies to the same input kind as the target row). Three practical lessons. **(a) Store the raw type text, not a classification** — the proof is then re-derived by whatever the current classifier says, instead of freezing today's implementation into last year's data. **(b) Two places drop the new field with no compiler error:** `ParameterHistoryStore.Record` rebuilds each `ParameterValue` member by member (a projection, not a copy), and `ValuesEqual` decides whether a run is a *repeat* — leaving the type out of that comparison means re-running the same text under a changed type refreshes the old entry and keeps its **stale** type, so the value the user just used could never be proven again. Row-level tests stay green through both. **(c) In `System.Text.Json` an optional field is additive in both directions** (unknown members are ignored on read), so **do not bump the schema version for one** — a bump trips downgrade protection and makes an older build refuse the entire file, a far bigger regression than the field is a feature. Legacy rows with no type simply cannot be proven and are not auto-applied, which self-heals on the next write. *(Debugger C3.2, 2026-07-25; `ParameterHistory.cs` / `ParameterHistoryStore.cs`; `docs/history/19-...`)*

258. **An event nobody subscribes to is a feature that silently does nothing — the compiler is happy, the tests are happy, and the only symptom is a user saying "it doesn't update".** `SourceObjectDetailTabViewModel`, `ViewDetailTabViewModel` and `PackageDetailTabViewModel` had all raised `CompiledExistingObject` after every successful compile of an existing object, for a long time, with **no subscriber anywhere in the app**. `?.Invoke()` on a null event is legal and free, so it ran on every compile and did nothing; the intended consumer (`OfferRecompileDependentsAsync`, the "recompile dependents?" offer) was itself never called, and the view's matching `RecompileDependentsRequested` handler waited for a notification that never came. Two features were dead at once and nothing anywhere reported it. **When you find an event declaration, grep for `+= ` on it before assuming the feature works**; when you *add* one, add the subscriber in the same commit or don't add the event. This is #233's cousin — a tested-but-uncalled component looks exactly like a regression — with the extra sting that an event needs no call site to look plausible. **The same class of silence hides in a hard-coded placeholder value**: the debugger tab set `ObjectKind = MetadataObjectKind.Procedure` for every routine, which was genuinely harmless *while nothing read it*, and became a wrong-object bug the moment a feature started keying on it. The counter-measure that worked: when adding a parameter whose value must be decided per call site, make it **required rather than optional with a default** — the compiler then enumerates every caller for you, which is exactly how both stale call sites surfaced instead of silently keeping the old value. *(Seam 6d, 2026-07-25; `MainWindowViewModel.WireObjectCompiled`; `docs/history/19-...`)*

259. **A `decimal`'s stored scale is NOT its precision — `1.50m` and `1.5m` are the same number with different scales, so any "would this lose digits?" check written against `decimal.GetBits` reports a loss on perfectly exact money data.** The import validator has to refuse a value carrying more decimals than the target column keeps (rounding `1.555` into `NUMERIC(15,2)` is precisely the silent conversion §0.1 forbids). The obvious implementation reads the scale out of `decimal.GetBits(d)[3]` and compares it to the column's — and it is wrong, because `decimal` preserves trailing zeros as part of its representation: `1.50m` has scale **2**, so it would be rejected by a `NUMERIC(15,1)` column that holds it exactly. Every price ending in a round cent would have become a row error. **Ask the question you actually mean: does rounding CHANGE the value?** — `Math.Round(value, scale, MidpointRounding.ToEven) != value`. `decimal`'s `==` compares numeric value and ignores trailing zeros, so `1.50m == 1.5m` is true and the check stays silent, while `1.555m` rounded to 2 really is a different number and is caught. Two edges worth keeping: `Math.Round` throws above 28 decimals, and the companion precision bound (`10^(precision-scale)`) is not representable past 28 either — in both cases **skip the check and let the engine decide**, because silence is honest and a wrong bound is not. The same trap sits in any "is this value exact enough for this column" logic — persistence, export, a UI validator — wherever `decimal` is the carrier. *(Data Import I2, 2026-07-26; `ImportRowValidator.ValidateDecimal`; `docs/design/data-import.md`)*

264. **`AsyncRelayCommand` rethrows a faulted command on the dispatcher, where nothing is left to catch it — so in an Avalonia app an unhandled exception inside a `[RelayCommand]` does not produce a bad result, it TERMINATES THE PROCESS.** Data Import I8 shipped with `catch (Exception ex) when (ex is InvalidOperationException or TimeoutException …)` at every point it called out to the world. Pressing Import on a new table closed the whole application: an `FbException` (SQLSTATE -204, *Table unknown*) walked straight past every one of those clauses, out of `RunAsync`, into `AsyncRelayCommand.AwaitAndThrowIfFailed`, onto the dispatcher, and out of the process. **The general rule: the outermost point of a user-initiated async operation needs a catch-ALL, because it is the only place that can honour "this feature may not kill the app".** ⚠ **And there is a second, sharper reason an allow-list was the wrong shape here, which generalises further: a component that reaches the world through DELEGATES cannot enumerate the exceptions the world throws.** The whole point of `DataImportEnvironment` is that no Firebird type reaches a ViewModel (rule #1) — but that erasure cuts both ways, and it makes `catch (X or Y)` unknowable by construction at exactly the boundaries that need it most. The module could not even *name* `DdlExecutionException` without violating its own layering. Wherever a type is deliberately hidden from you, catch by POSITION (this is the boundary) rather than by TYPE. Keep `OperationCanceledException` separate and above it — a cancel is a decision, not a fault (#253). **Two smaller traps from the same bug.** (a) A **fire-and-forget** task (here the recalculation chain, held only as `PendingRecalculation`) turns an escaping exception into an `UnobservedTaskException` rethrown by the finalizer thread — same fatal outcome, arriving later and from nowhere. Guard it too. (b) The app's `AppDomain.UnhandledException` hook wrote the whole stack trace to `%TEMP%\EmberTern-debug.log`, which is what turned "it just closes" into a one-minute diagnosis. **Read that log FIRST when a crash is reported** — do not start by re-reading code. *(Data Import I8 review, 2026-07-27; `DataImportTabViewModel.RunGuardedAsync`)*

265. **Hiding a control does not retract the decision it carries — the value stays in the model, invisible, and the next code path to read it is the one that breaks.** The same I8 crash had a second cause underneath the exception handling. "Empty the table before importing" is meaningless for a table the import is about to CREATE, so the surface hides that checkbox in the new-table variant — but `BuildBehavior` still copied `EmptyTargetBeforeImport = EmptyBeforeImport` unconditionally. A user who had the box ticked for an existing table (or, more likely, whose **restored "last used" configuration** carried it — the tick then arrives automatically and is never seen at all) switched to "new table" and left `true` sitting in the record. The run duly tried to `SELECT COUNT(*)` from a table that did not exist yet. **`IsVisible` is presentation; the record is the decision, and only the record's own producer can withdraw one.** The fix belongs in the ONE place that turns UI state into the record (`BuildBehavior` → `!IsNewTable && EmptyBeforeImport`), not in the consumer — a guard at the call site would be a second opinion about whose decision it is. ⚠ Note what is deliberately NOT done: the checkbox's own value is not cleared, so switching back finds the tick where the user left it. **What must not survive is a decision that does not apply, not the user's preference.** A useful smell for the future: any time a control gains an `IsVisible` binding on a mode, ask what its value does in the mode where it is hidden. *(Data Import I8 review, 2026-07-27; `ImportTargetSectionViewModel.BuildBehavior`)*

262. **A component that INFERS a type and a component that CONVERTS a value must be the same code asking the same question — two of them is a timebomb whose fuse is the length of the file.** Data Import's I8 has to propose a column type for a table it is about to create. The obvious shape is a small type sniffer: does this text look like an integer, a date, a decimal. It is also wrong, and the failure mode is nasty rather than noisy: the sniffer types a column `INTEGER`, the table is **created and COMMITTED on the Ddl lane** (it has to be — gotcha #213), the import starts, and somewhere in the middle a value the sniffer would have accepted is refused by the *converter*, which has its own slightly different rules about thousands separators, leading signs or date field order. The user is left with a half-filled, committed, orphaned table. **The fix is not to make the two agree; it is to have only one.** `ColumnTypeInferencer` answers every "could this value be an X?" by calling `ImportValueConverter.Convert(value, probeTypeForX, culture)` — the very method that will convert the value during the real run, under the very same `ImportCultureOptions`. Inference and conversion cannot drift because there is nothing to drift from. Pinned by a test that re-feeds every observed value through the converter against the type that was proposed for it. **The general rule: when component A decides what B will be asked to do, A must ask B rather than model B.** A modelled B is a copy, and a copy is a thing that gets edited once. ⚠ Two corollaries worth keeping. (a) **The probe type must be the WIDEST form of the candidate** (`BIGINT`, `NUMERIC(18,4)`), so a value is rejected for its SHAPE and not for a width the inferencer has not chosen yet — the declared width is decided once, at the end, from what was actually seen. (b) **Some rejections belong to §0.1 rather than to parsing, and the converter cannot make them for you**: `007` parses as 7 perfectly, but the integer and the text are different data (a postal code, an index, an account number), so a significant leading zero disqualifies the number candidate *on top of* what the converter says. That rule lives in the inferencer because it is a question about faithfulness, not about parsing. *(Data Import I8, 2026-07-27; `ColumnTypeInferencer`, `ImportValueConverter`; design R19 / §0.1 / §0.3)*

263. **When one artefact is going to be produced twice — once as a preview and once for real — generate both from the same call, or the preview is a drawing of the thing rather than the thing.** Data Import I8 shows the user the `CREATE TABLE` it is about to run, validates the file against those columns before anything is created, and then actually creates them. That is **three** consumers of "what type is this column", and the tempting shape is a display formatter for the preview plus a DDL builder for the run. `ImportNewTable` makes all three the same string: `TypeText`, `BuildCreateSql` and `Project` (which turns the definitions into an `ImportTarget` so the dry run can measure the file against a table that does not exist) every one route through `DdlGenerator.FormatTypeOrDomain`. The payoff is not elegance — it is that a divergence here would surface as **rows rejected by a table the module itself designed**, which reads to the user as the import being broken rather than the preview being wrong. ⚠ **And the projection is still only a prediction**: after the real `CREATE` the coordinator re-reads the target **from the catalog**, because Firebird is the authority on what it built. A live probe case asserts the two agree (the catalog returned `VARCHAR(2), VARCHAR(8), NUMERIC(4,2), DATE` — exactly what was asked for); asserting that against our own parser instead would have been asking the question of the code that produced the answer. ⚠ Watch the shape mismatch when converting between column models: `ImportColumnDefinition` carries ONE `Size` while the shared `FieldDefinition` splits it into `Size` (text length) and `Precision` (numeric) — feeding the wrong slot emits `NUMERIC` with no precision at all, and the conversion is therefore allowed in exactly one method. *(Data Import I8, 2026-07-27; `ImportNewTable`, `DdlGenerator`; design §4.6 / §0.5)*

260. **One error condition can reach you under more than one GDS code, and a probe that exercised only the common form will tell you it cannot — measuring "a UNIQUE constraint" is not measuring "uniqueness".** The Data Import error mapper was built on I0's measured table, which listed a primary-key violation and a UNIQUE violation as the same vector (`335544665`) and concluded the two were interchangeable. They are. What I0 never created was a **standalone `CREATE UNIQUE INDEX`** — and violating one of those leads with a *different* code, `335544349` (`isc_no_dup`, *"attempt to store duplicate value … in unique index"*), so the import reported an ordinary duplicate key as a generic `ServerError`. The earlier measurement was correct about what it measured and silent about the rest, and no amount of re-reading it would have shown that; only running the production mapper against a live engine did. **Two rules follow.** (a) When a probe enumerates "the error classes", check what SCHEMA it built — a class exercised through one DDL form has been measured for that form only, and constraints, standalone indexes, domain-level CHECKs and trigger-raised failures are different forms of the same idea. (b) Keep the classifier's mapping many-to-one and say so: both codes mean "this value is already there", so both become one kind, and the report never claims to know which mechanism enforced it. A second, related trap from the same session: **once client-side validation lands, whole families of server errors become unreachable** — the import validates NOT NULL, length and numeric range before the round trip, so those server branches could not be exercised live at all until the lab gained a trigger (`IMP_SRV_BI`) that manufactures them *inside* the engine. A defensive branch nobody can reach is a branch nobody has tested (#233's cousin). *(Data Import I4, 2026-07-26; `FirebirdImportErrorMapper`; `tools/probes/DataImportProbe`; `docs/design/data-import-i0-findings.md` §2.6)*

266. **A guard added where a problem was first felt is not a guard on the problem — `SidebarFlatController` had owned an explicit `BeginUpdate`/`EndUpdate` bulk guard for a long time, and it was wired to exactly ONE of the two paths that perform a mass mutation.** The comment on it even names the failure it prevents (*"an O(n²) storm"*), and it was applied around the FILTER rebuild. The other mass mutation — `MetadataNodeViewModel.SetLeaves`, which replaces a category's leaves with `Children.Clear()` then one `Add` per object — ran unguarded, so every single `Add` re-spliced the owner's whole child block while the category was expanded. **Measured on the real controller at production scale (2 400 tables): 5.76 million row operations and ~1 200 ms on the UI thread for one category; 1 424 ms for a whole `RefreshAsync` with a single category expanded; 2 ms with the guard.** Nobody had to invent anything — the fix was to call the existing pair from `LoadGroupAsync`, `RefreshAsync` and the connect-time prefetch. **Two things generalise.** (a) When you find a suppression/batching primitive, grep for its call sites and then ask what OTHER operations have the same shape; a primitive with one caller is usually a bug report waiting, not a design. (b) The cost was invisible in every ordinary signal — build green, tests green, smoke clean — because it is a **cost**, not a state; only a probe that drove the real controller at real scale showed it (`tools/probes/MetadataPerfProbe`). ⚠ Know the trade-off you are accepting: `EndUpdate` re-projects the whole list, so row objects are recreated and the ListBox scrolls to the top. That was already true of every refresh (it ended in `ApplyFilterAsync`, which does exactly this), but it broke one probe that had captured a `SidebarRow` **instance** across a connect — a test asserting a projection's mirroring should re-resolve the row, not hold it. *(Metadata refresh — Layer 1, 2026-07-27; `docs/design/metadata-refresh-analysis.md` §7)*

267. **When the code that changed the world already knows WHAT it changed, make it say so — "refresh yourself" is the expensive way to ask someone to rediscover a fact you were holding.** Data Import created a table and the metadata tree did not show it, because the module never told the tree anything; the twenty existing DDL paths all call `Metadata.RefreshAsync()`, and adding a twenty-first would have been correct in a minute. It would also have cost **13 catalog queries (~164 ms) plus a full re-projection of every expanded category** to discover one table whose name was sitting in a local variable. Instead the module reports a fact — `TableCreated(name)` / `TableDropped(name)` — and the explorer inserts or removes **one leaf at its sorted position** (1.3 ms, no round trip). Note the framing: the delegate is a **statement** ("this table now exists"), not a **command** ("refresh"), which is what keeps the knowledge with the party that has it and the tree policy with the tree. **Three details that make a targeted update actually correct**, and each is a place a naive version leaks: (a) it must be **idempotent** — a later refresh may already have picked the object up, so check before inserting; (b) an **active filter** means `Children` holds only matches, so the new leaf goes into the master list always and into the displayed list only if it matches, and the category's match count and zero-match visibility both have to be re-derived; (c) a category that is **not loaded** has no leaves to insert into but does have an `(N)` label on screen, so bump the count instead. ⚠ Keep the scope honest: this is one narrow precedent (the sibling of the existing `ApplyTriggerActiveStateInPlace`), **not** a general change protocol — generalising "added / removed / altered" across all twenty DDL paths, with incremental splicing that preserves scroll and selection, is a separate infrastructure stage. *(Data Import I8 review, 2026-07-27; `MetadataExplorerViewModel.ApplyObjectAddedInPlace`; `docs/design/metadata-refresh-analysis.md` §7)*

268. **A probe proves what it happened to execute, not what it was written to check — its PASS is evidence about the input, not about the heuristic.** Data Import's I0 probe had to answer "is this number a date?", which in a workbook is decided solely by the cell's number format. It asked the three-line version: strip quotes, lower-case, `code.Contains('y') || Contains('d') || Contains('h') || Contains('s')`. Run against the custom format found in the user's own real file — `#,##0\ [$€-1];[Red]\-#,##0\ [$€-1]`, which the same findings document labels **"currency, not a date"** — that test answers **TRUE**, because `[Red]` contains a `d`. The probe reported "numeric cells with a date format: **0**" and everyone read it as confirmation, when it only meant *no cell in that file used that style*. Copied into production it would have turned a money column into dates, silently, on the first file that did use it — §0.1's worst class, and the measurement that was supposed to prevent guessing would have been the source of the guess. **Three rules follow.** (a) When a probe's output is a COUNT and the count is zero, ask whether zero means "the condition is absent" or "the code path never ran" — they are indistinguishable in the output and opposite in meaning. (b) A heuristic that a probe carries is **not** measured merely because the probe passed; it is measured only if the probe fed it the hard case, and the hard case is usually the one already sitting in the real data. (c) Where the format being parsed has structure, parse it: the production version walks the code, skipping quoted literals, `\`-escapes and bracketed sections (`[Red]`, `[$€-1]`, `[<100]` are decoration; `[h]`/`[mm]`/`[ss]` are genuine elapsed-time tokens), and looks at the first `;`-section only. ⚠ Related, from the same reading: **Excel's calendar is not OLE's below 1900-03-01** — serial 1 is 1900-01-01 in Excel and 1899-12-31 to `DateTime.FromOADate`, and Excel additionally carries the phantom 1900-02-29 that never existed. `FromOADate` on a raw serial therefore shifts every January/February 1900 date by exactly one day, with no error; the phantom day is refused and left as a number rather than named as a date it cannot be. *(Data Import I9, 2026-07-27; `SpreadsheetNumberFormats`, `WorkbookCellReader.FromSerial`; `docs/design/data-import-i0-findings.md` §3.1/REK-6)*

269. **When a library hands you an already-decoded value, it has silently taken over a decision you may already own elsewhere — re-ask the question rather than accept the answer.** Data Import's I10 added a second spreadsheet reader (`.xls` via ExcelDataReader) beside I9's `.xlsx` one. The `.xlsx` path receives a bare serial and must decide, from the cell's number format, whether `45000` is a date — a decision that lives in exactly one place (`SpreadsheetNumberFormats`, which **parses** the format code rather than searching it; #268). ExcelDataReader is more helpful: it applies the format itself and hands back a `DateTime`. Accepting that is the obvious code and the wrong code, because it makes a **second** component the authority on one question, and the two will disagree on precisely the hard case #268 is about — the user's real custom currency format `#,##0\ [$€-1];[Red]\-#,##0\ [$€-1]`, which our own owner rejects and the library independently accepts. A money column becoming a date column, in one of two providers only, is a bug nobody would think to look for. So the provider asks `GetNumberFormatIndex`/`GetNumberFormatString` and puts the answer back through **our** owner, keeping the library's decoded value only once ours agrees. ⚠ **The mirror-image hazard is worth naming, because it is the one that catches people:** where the raw format makes you afraid of a date being MISSED, a decoding library makes you afraid of a date being INVENTED. ⚠ **And a second owner arrives with it: the calendar.** Honouring "give me the serial, not the date" now requires converting *back*, so the epoch fact exists in both directions — and an inverse written beside a forward function it cannot see is how two halves of one calendar drift apart. Extracting `ExcelSerialDate` (`FromSerial` + `ToSerial`) fixed a divergence that already existed unnoticed: a "time only" cell came out of `.xlsx` as a `TimeSpan` and out of `.xls` as `1899-12-31 12:00`. **General rule: a convenience the library performs for you is a responsibility it has taken from you; find out which of your own owners it has displaced before you accept the result.** *(Data Import I10, 2026-07-27; `XlsCellReader.Normalize`, `ExcelSerialDate`; `docs/design/data-import.md`)*

270. **A probe must walk the WHOLE road the product walks, or a green product will look broken — and the instinct to "fix" the product is exactly backwards.** Data Import's live probe gained a case for importing a paste from Excel: TAB-separated text, no file on disk. It set `AutoDetectDelimiter = true` on the configuration, handed it to `ImportPipeline`, and got back **one column** and zero rows written. Nothing was wrong. Detection is deliberately a step **above** the pipeline: §0.4 lets auto-detection PROPOSE and forbids it from deciding silently, so the surface runs `DelimiterDetector`, shows the evidence to the user, and writes the **resolved** separator into the configuration — which is why a provider reads a declared delimiter and holds no opinion about detection at all. The probe had skipped the layer where the decision is made and then asked the layer below to make it. **The diagnostic rule: when a probe and the running UI disagree about the same input, first establish that the probe reproduced every step the UI performs; only then look for a defect in the product.** A probe that starts one layer too low does not test less, it tests something else — and it reports the difference as a product failure, which is the most expensive possible way to be wrong. ⚠ The general shape to watch for: any behaviour where a value is *proposed by one layer and consumed by another* (detection, defaulting, resolution, normalisation) has a seam a test can accidentally start below. *(Data Import I10, 2026-07-27; `tools/probes/DataImportRunProbe` section I; `DataImportTabViewModel.RunDetectionAsync`)*

271. **A fetch that delivers its result by ASSIGNING a property is not a re-read — an unchanged value raises nothing, so "read it again" silently recomputes nothing at all.** Data Import's clipboard source was fetched by a command: read the text, assign it to `Source.ClipboardText`, and let the property change trigger the recalculation chain. It worked the first time and looked like it always would. It does not: `SetProperty` compares, and identical text means no `PropertyChanged`, no `Changed`, no chain — so re-reading a clipboard whose content had not changed recomputed **nothing**, and the user reported it as "even Ctrl+V does not rebuild the mapping". Worse, the shape had made the clipboard the one source with its own way in: every other source was read *by* the chain, this one was read *beside* it and pushed in. **The fix is to move the fetch INTO the pipeline that consumes it and trigger the pipeline explicitly** — then "what happens next" is a consequence of having been asked, not of the value differing. ⚠ Two corollaries. (a) A link that writes into the state the chain watches must **suspend** that notification (the same guard a detector's proposal uses) or the chain restarts itself. (b) Once the chain owns the read, "may I read it?" needs a **reason** — an argument on the one chain, never a second chain: here `ImportChainTrigger.Decision` (an edit — keep the cached facts) vs `Refresh` (the user asked — re-read them), which is the same discipline that made "Validate" a different *writer* passed to the one pipeline rather than a second mode. **The general smell: any state whose only refresh trigger is "someone assigns it" cannot be refreshed to its current value.** *(Data Import — post-I10 ergonomics seam, 2026-07-27; `DataImportTabViewModel.ReadClipboardIfNeededAsync`)*

272. **A descriptor with no way to say "nothing chosen yet" will answer with one of its real values — and every guard written against that value then fires in a state nobody meant.** `SourceDescriptor` distinguishes a file (kind + path) from the clipboard (kind, no path), and `BuildSource` reads `UseFile && FilePath.Length > 0 ? File(...) : Clipboard()`. So **"File is selected but no file has been picked"** reports `Kind == Clipboard`. Harmless for years, because the only consumer asked "is there a source to read?" and the clipboard's text was empty anyway. It stopped being harmless the moment the recalculation chain grew an *automatic* clipboard read gated on `Kind == Clipboard`: every freshly opened import tab would have reached for the clipboard and, whenever the content happened to look tabular, adopted it as the source **while the „File" radio was still selected** — data appearing from nowhere under the wrong label. The fix is to gate on the thing that actually carries the user's choice (`Source.UseFile`, i.e. the radio) rather than on a derived value that conflates two states. **Rule: before writing a condition on an enum-ish descriptor, enumerate the states the type cannot express and find out which real value each of them is reported as.** The tell is a producer with an `X && Y ? A : B` shape — `B` is doing double duty. It surfaced here as a test counting clipboard reads and finding two where one was expected; without the counter it would have shipped. *(Data Import — post-I10 ergonomics seam, 2026-07-27; `ImportSourceSectionViewModel.BuildSource`)*

273. **A navigation affordance that resolves its target by "first focusable control" silently does nothing wherever that control is DISABLED — and a resolution allowed to return null turns a working feature into a dead one without a single failing signal.** Data Import's readiness strip gives each section a chip that takes the user to it. Two of the five were reported as *"feeling like dummies"*, and they were: **Target** resolved to the existing-table picker, which is disabled whenever the „new table" variant is selected, and Avalonia's `IsEffectivelyEnabled` is exactly what a `FirstFocusable` helper filters on — so it returned `null` and the click was swallowed. **Mapping** resolved to "the row needing attention", which is `null` once everything is mapped, so that chip worked *only while something was wrong*. Nothing threw, nothing logged, every property was healthy, and the suite was green: focus that goes nowhere produces no observable failure. Three rules came out of it. **(a) A resolution used by a user-facing affordance must not be able to produce nothing** — give every branch a fallback chain ending at the section's own container, so the worst case is "took you to roughly the right place" rather than "did nothing". **(b) `Focus()` scrolls nothing.** A control below the fold takes focus invisibly; pair it with `BringIntoView()` **before** focusing, or a long list makes a working jump look dead. **(c) "Which control is this section currently about" is a VIEWMODEL decision when it depends on state the VM owns** — here the chosen target variant. The view had been inferring it, which is a second opinion about which half of a section is live; moving it to the VM (`ImportTargetFocus`) both fixed the defect and made it testable, and the *absence of that answer* was the whole bug. ⚠ The general tell: any `switch` mapping a logical destination to a control, where some destinations are conditionally disabled or conditionally present. Read every arm asking "in which state is this control absent, disabled, or collapsed?" — those are the states where the feature quietly stops existing. *(Data Import — I11 second review, 2026-07-27; `DataImportTabView.ResolveSectionTarget`, `DataImportTabViewModel.TargetFocus`)*

274. **A `*` row in a Grid whose siblings are all `Auto` has no floor: "the star gets what is left" includes getting nothing — and the panels it exists to protect then disappear while every binding, command and test stays healthy.** Data Import's surface is eight `Auto` bands and one star row holding Mapping + the converted preview, with the layout comment explicitly declaring that *"the star belongs to the work, not to the configuration"*. In the „new table" variant the Target tile — type grid plus an optional DDL preview — grows into the tallest band on the screen, and the bottom panel below it holds an **absolute** pixel height, so the star's share went to zero: both work panels vanished. The visible symptom was blamed on something else entirely (a readiness chip "pointing at a dummy"), and it was chased and "fixed" once at that level before anyone measured the row — the chip was resolving perfectly onto a panel with no height. **Two rules.** **(a) A star row that carries the work needs `MinHeight`, and the declaration is only half of it — a floor nothing can reach is not a floor.** With every sibling `Auto` and one sibling fixed, honouring the floor pushes the last rows off the bottom; something must be told to yield, and the right candidate is the band whose height *the program* chose (here the resizable bottom panel), never the content-sized ones. Clamp it rather than overwriting its stored value, so the user's dragged height returns by itself when the tall band shrinks. **(b) Re-clamp when an `Auto` band grows, not only when the window resizes** — choosing a variant with forty columns changes no window size, only how much room is left; subscribing `SizeChanged` on the protected element itself is the precise trigger, and assigning an unchanged `GridLength` is a no-op so it settles in one pass instead of oscillating. ⚠ The general tell: a layout comment that promises which region is *important*, sitting above `RowDefinitions` where that region is the only `*` among `Auto`s. The promise is unenforced until a floor exists. ⚠ **POSTSCRIPT (2026-07-27, same day): the FIX was reverted by the user; the diagnosis was not.** Seen running, honouring the floor forced the bottom panel to give way — the middle of the window grew and the bottom panel stopped being useful. At the application’s current control heights you cannot have both, so the floor was removed and the real repair moved to the app-wide UX sprint, where control heights come down and the surface regains the room. **The lesson survives and is the more useful half: a `*` row among `Auto` siblings has no floor, and you should know that before a user reports a vanished panel. But adding the floor is only correct if some band can actually yield — and when the only candidate is a panel the user relies on, the honest answer is that the layout is over-subscribed, not that the star row needs a minimum.** Ordering matters too: a floor is a fix for a surface that has room; density is a fix for one that does not. ⭐⭐ **AND THE REAL CAUSE WAS NEITHER.** The user found it after both attempts failed: the work area held **Mapping (a decision) beside the converted preview (a result)**, while the type grid — also a decision — sat above in an `Auto` band. Configuration and outcome were interleaved, so every answer phrased in pixels had to be a compromise between things that should not have been competing at all. Moving the preview into the bottom panel, where results already live, and giving the work area entirely to configuration, dissolved the symptom with no floor and no extra section. **The general rule, and it is the useful one: when two panels fight over space, ask first whether they belong to the same CATEGORY OF RESPONSIBILITY — a decision and its consequence do not — because a bad allocation cannot be fixed by resizing.** *(Data Import — I11 third review, added then reverted, then dissolved by the configuration/results split, 2026-07-27; `DataImportTabView.axaml` SurfaceLayout, `ApplyBottomPanel`/`ClampBottomHeight`)*

275. **A module that CHANGES a fact it also caches must tell its own cache — telling the rest of the application is not the same thing, and the second symptom is usually worse than the reported one.** Data Import caches the database's table list (it is a fact about the world, read once per tab, not on every keystroke) and its „new table" variant creates tables. On a successful `CREATE` it announced the fact — to the **metadata tree** (`TableCreated`), which duly grew a leaf — and said nothing to itself. The reported symptom was cosmetic: the freshly created table was missing from the module's own „Existing table" picker until the tab was reopened. The unreported one was not: the same cached list backs `IsNewTableNameTaken`, so **`IMP0028` stopped being able to see that the name was taken**, and re-running the very same import produced a **green readiness strip followed by a raw server error** — precisely the state that diagnostic was written to prevent, and its own comment says so. **Two rules.** **(a) When you notify the world about a state change, enumerate your OWN readers of that state first** — the outward notification is the visible one and therefore the one you remember; a component that both caches and mutates is the one that forgets. Grep for every reader of the cached field, not just for the event. **(b) A cache whose owner knows the changed key should be PATCHED, not invalidated** — here the module knows the table's name, so inserting it at its sorted position costs nothing, while dropping the cache would buy a catalog round trip to re-learn a name already in hand (the same reasoning as `MetadataExplorerViewModel.ApplyObjectAddedInPlace`). Report a fact („this table exists"), never a command („refresh"), and keep the operation idempotent — an explicit refresh may already have brought it in. ⚠ Symmetry is part of the fix: a cache that learns about creations but not deletions offers a target that no longer exists. *(Data Import — I12, 2026-07-27; `DataImportTabViewModel.CreateTargetTableAsync`, `ImportTargetSectionViewModel.NoteTableExists`/`NoteTableGone`)*

276. **A probe that writes bulk data into the COMMITTED lab database bloats the repository, and emptying the table afterwards does not shrink it back.** The I12 million-row measurement ran against `Lab/EmberTern_Lab.fdb`, which is committed to Git on purpose, and ended by emptying `IMP_TARGET`. Logically the database was back where it started; physically it had gone **4 MB → 120 MB**, because Firebird allocates pages for the rows and a `DELETE` releases them for reuse *inside the file* rather than returning them to the filesystem. Nothing warns you: `git status` reports the `.fdb` as "modified", exactly as it would after a one-row change, and committing it would have added 116 MB of allocated emptiness to the repo's history forever. **The rule: after any probe that writes at scale to the lab, `git restore Lab/EmberTern_Lab.fdb`** — the probe resets its own table, so the committed file is already the correct logical state, and restoring is a no-op semantically and a 116 MB saving physically. Check the size (`ls -l` against `git cat-file -s HEAD:Lab/EmberTern_Lab.fdb`) rather than trusting that a reset undid the growth. *(Data Import — I12 million-row measurement, 2026-07-27)*

277. **A `Stretch`-aligned control that cannot use all its space is CENTRED in it — so a stale `MaxHeight` shows up as blank bands above AND below the content, which reads like "space reserved for something else" rather than like a size cap.** Data Import's type grid was moved from a fixed-height `Auto` band into a star column so it could fill the work area, and the comment written beside it said the cap was gone. It was not: `MaxHeight="170"` survived the move. The symptom pointed away from the cause — the grid floated in the middle of its panel with empty strips on either side, and the user reasonably read it as "the layout is still holding room for the DDL preview". Every property involved was correct, nothing overflowed, and the build was clean. **Two rules.** **(a) When a control looks vertically centred inside a container it should be filling, look for a `MaxHeight`/`MaxWidth`/`Height` on it before you look at the container** — under `Stretch`, an unusable remainder is distributed evenly, which is exactly the visual signature of reserved space. **(b) ⚠ A comment claiming something was removed is not evidence that it was.** The comment here was written in the same edit that was *supposed* to remove the cap and was believed on re-reading; it turned a five-second grep into a user-reported layout bug. Verify the code, not the prose next to it — especially your own prose, and especially when the claim is "no longer". *(Data Import — the configuration/results split, 2026-07-27; `DataImportTabView.axaml` TypesPanel)*

278. **A transitive dependency's THREAT MODEL changes when the direction of use changes, and nobody re-runs the assessment — the package version stays identical while the exposure does not.** `DocumentFormat.OpenXml` pulls `System.IO.Packaging 8.0.0`, which carries two **High** advisories (CVE-2024-43483 hash flooding, CVE-2024-43484 algorithmic complexity), both denial of service **on hostile input**, both fixed in 8.0.1. A 2026-07-26 audit found them and softened the finding with a reasonable-sounding argument: *"the current Office module exports, and does not import, XLSX"* — no untrusted workbook, no exposure. That was true when written and **already false when read**: etap I9 had added `XlsxImportProvider`, so the module's whole purpose now includes parsing `.xlsx` files the user was handed by someone else. "Hostile input" had become the ordinary use case, and the only thing that changed was a feature, not a version. **The rule: re-run a dependency's threat assessment whenever the module using it gains a direction of data flow, not only when the version moves** — an SCA scan reports versions, and a version that has not changed produces no signal at all. ⚠ **Two mechanical notes from fixing it.** (a) Overriding a transitive pin means adding a **direct** `PackageReference`, which also brings the package under NuGet's `NU1902`/`NU1903` — and with `TreatWarningsAsErrors=true` a newly-disclosed advisory then **fails the build** instead of being a scan footnote. That is the desired behaviour, and it is why the pinned version must be the genuinely patched one: `SixLabors.ImageSharp` needed **2.1.11**, not the 2.1.10 that fixes only the first of its two advisories. (b) An **XML comment cannot contain a double hyphen**, so a `.csproj` comment documenting the fix cannot quote the `dotnet list package` flags that find it. *(Architecture Hardening sprint / A-05, 2026-07-27; `EmberTern.Office.csproj`, `EmberTern.Tests.csproj`)*

279. **A reconstruction that SYNTHESIZES a plausible result for a missing object cannot answer "does this object exist" — and a fingerprint over what it returns is the right change-detector precisely because it is the wrong existence test.** The DDL change-safety gate needed both questions. `FirebirdDdlReader.BuildProcedureDdlAsync` rebuilds a routine's `CREATE OR ALTER` from the catalog, and for a name with no row it does not return empty — it emits a well-formed stub with a `/* Procedure source not available. */` body (**measured: 106 characters for a dropped procedure**). So the obvious design — "no definition therefore the name is free" — would have reported every non-existent object as existing, and inverted the New-object guard into a permanent refusal. Existence had to come from a separate catalog check (`FirebirdMetadataReader.ExistsAsync`), built over the **same `ListAsync` the object tree uses**, because "does this name exist" carries the per-kind table choice, the `SYSTEM_FLAG` predicate, the client-side system-name filter and the FB3+ exclusion of packaged routines — a second query would be a second definition of existence, free to drift, and a gate that disagrees with the tree about what exists is worse than no gate. **The complementary half is what makes the same reconstruction the right thing to fingerprint:** it is rebuilt from parameters *and* body, so it detects a **signature-only** change that a fingerprint over `RDB$PROCEDURE_SOURCE` alone would miss entirely (live-verified: changing `P_IN INTEGER` to `BIGINT` with a byte-identical body changes the fingerprint). **General rule: before using a "read it back" function as a presence test, find out what it returns for absence — a function written to always produce usable output has been designed never to say "nothing".** ⚠ And verify the determinism the comparison rests on rather than assuming it: two reads of an unchanged routine are byte-identical (proven over 20 consecutive reads on FB5), which is the single fact that separates this gate from one that reports a false conflict on every compile. *(Architecture Hardening sprint / A-01, 2026-07-27; `ObjectChangeSafety`, `tools/probes/ChangeSafetyProbe` 19/19)*

280. **A load that returns `null` for both "nothing saved yet" and "saved, but I cannot read it" is a data-loss vector the moment any caller answers `null` with a default and writes it back — and the doc comment claiming otherwise is part of the bug.** `ApplicationSettingsStore.Load()` degraded to `null` for a missing file AND for an undecryptable one; all eight section facades do `Load() ?? new ApplicationSettings()` followed by `Save()`; and the save-side guard `ExistingFileIsFromFuture` contained an explicit `catch (Exception) { return false; }` commented *"Corrupt / undecryptable with a known scheme → not a future file; allow the replace"*. On any machine where DPAPI cannot decrypt — a copied Windows profile, a restored account — the next write replaced connection profiles, passwords, saved queries, workspace and watch expressions with defaults. **The triggering writes are the ones nobody thinks of as writes**: a grid column resized, a procedure run recording its parameters, the app closing. Meanwhile the class comment asserted *"Load degrades to null in that case and never overwrites the unreadable file"* — literally true of `Load`, and precisely the sentence that stops a reader from checking `Save`. **Three rules.** (a) **A load result must distinguish absence from failure** whenever anything writes back a default; the fix is a status (`Missing`/`Loaded`/`Unreadable`/`Corrupt`/`FutureVersion`), not a null. (b) **Classify by cause, because the causes have different prognoses** — an undecryptable file is usually *intact data belonging to another account* and is the one most worth not destroying, whereas a zero-length file holds nothing and is safe to replace. (c) The old reasoning — *"never strand the user forever on a genuinely broken file"* — had the trade-off backwards: being stranded is **visible and recoverable**, the overwrite it permitted was **silent and final**. Give the stranded user an explicit, backup-taking escape (`SaveOverUnreadableFile`) instead of pre-authorising the destruction. ⚠ **And refusing quietly is only half an obligation:** with saves refused, nothing the user does persists, so the App must SAY so — otherwise the safe behaviour is indistinguishable from working. ⚠ A doc comment asserting a safety property is a claim to verify, not evidence (the same trap as #277, one layer up: here the prose described the neighbouring method's behaviour and was believed for the whole class). *(Architecture Hardening sprint / A-03, 2026-07-27; `SettingsLoadResult`, `ApplicationSettingsStore.ExistingFileBlocksSave`)*

281. **After you write, re-READ to re-establish a baseline — the text you SENT is not what the store gives back, so fingerprinting what you sent makes the next write look like somebody else's change.** The debugger already keeps a `_baseline` for the Draft model, and adding change-safety to its Save looked like a one-line reuse of it. It is not, and the difference is load-bearing: `_baseline` answers *"is the edit buffer dirty"*, and after a successful Save it deliberately becomes **the text that was sent** (which is correct for dirtiness — the buffer now matches what was compiled). A change-safety baseline has to hold **what the database RETURNS when read**, which for a routine is a catalog *reconstruction* in its own canonical layout, not the user's typing. Capture the sent text and every *second* Save is refused as a phantom conflict. So the gate re-reads through the same provider that armed it, and a failed re-read leaves it **disarmed rather than stale** — the next Save then says it could not verify, which is honest, instead of authorising an overwrite nobody checked. **General rule: when one field is asked to be "the last known good value" for two different questions, check whether the two questions are satisfied by the same artifact.** The tell is a field whose write sites are not all the same kind of event — here one site was a read from the store and the other was a write to it. ⚠ The object editors avoid this by accident rather than by design: their Compile ends in `RefreshAsync()`, which re-reads and therefore re-baselines for free. Anything that writes WITHOUT a reload afterwards has to do it explicitly. *(Architecture Hardening sprint / A-01, 2026-07-27; `DebuggerTabViewModel.CaptureChangeBaselineAsync`)*

282. **Inherited framework lore expires, and the cheapest way to find out is to enumerate what the control actually claims — not to read the library that inspired it.** The Keyboard Manager sprint's whole shortcut map is F-key-first, so the first question was which F-keys AvaloniaEdit already owns. Every source of received wisdom says `F3`/`Shift+F3` are find-next/find-previous inside an AvalonEdit-derived editor. **Measured on AvaloniaEdit 12.0.0: they are not.** `SearchInputHandler` registers `Find` / `Replace` / `FindNext` / `FindPrevious` / `ReplaceNext` / `ReplaceAll` / `CloseSearchPanel` as `CommandBindings` with **no `KeyGesture` at all**, and both `TextEditor.KeyBindings` and `TextArea.KeyBindings` are empty — so of the 39 gestures reachable from a live editor, **zero** are function keys. What it *does* claim is the editing set: `Delete`, `Ctrl+Delete`, `Back`, `Shift+Back`, `Ctrl+Back`, `Return`, `Shift+Return`, the arrows with `Shift`/`Ctrl`, and `Shift+Alt`+arrows for box-selection. **Three rules.** **(a) Enumerate, don't recall** — an input handler chain is walkable in a dozen lines of reflection (`TextArea.DefaultInputHandler` → `NestedInputHandlers` → each handler's `KeyBindings`), and the answer arrives in one test run instead of a design argument. **(b) Grepping the assembly is not enough**: the command *names* are in the metadata strings, so `FindNext` is findable and looks like confirmation — but the gestures are constructed in IL, so their absence is invisible to a string search. Only a live instance answers. **(c) Keep the enumeration as a test, not a throwaway probe.** The risk it guards is silent in the worst way: an upgrade that starts binding a function key steals a global shortcut with the build green and every test passing. ⚠ Corollary for the shortcut map: `Delete` may never be a global gesture (the editor owns it), which is why "F8 = Delete" is the right shape and "Delete = Delete" is not; and `Shift+Alt` being box-selection is a second, independent reason to keep Alt out of application gestures. *(Keyboard Manager — etap 1 audit + etap 2 pin, 2026-07-28; `ConnectionExpandBindingProbe.Editor_ClaimsNoFunctionKey_AndClaimsTheEditingKeys`)*

283. **A control that already creates a collaborator in its constructor will happily let you install a SECOND one — and the duplicate is invisible because both halves work, just not on the same object.** `AvaloniaEdit.TextEditor` builds and installs its own `SearchPanel` (measured: a bare editor already reports one `SearchInputHandler` and a non-null `TextEditor.SearchPanel`). `EditorSearch.Install` then called `SearchPanel.Install(editor)` on top of it, which registered a **second** handler and returned a **different** panel — `ReferenceEquals(editor.SearchPanel, returned) == false`. So `Ctrl+F` drove the editor's own panel while the right-click menu's Find/Replace and the hand-wired `Ctrl+H` drove the other one: two search bars, two states, one visible surface, and a class doc-comment asserting *"Find via Ctrl+F is auto-wired by `SearchPanel.Install`"* that made the arrangement read as deliberate. Nothing threw, nothing looked wrong in isolation, and the neighbouring test asserted only that the returned panel was **non-null** — which passed identically before and after the fix. **Two rules.** **(a) Before calling a static `Install`/`Attach`/`Register` on a third-party control, check whether the control already did it** — the tell is an `Install` that *returns* the thing it installs while the control also *exposes* it as a property; if both exist, one of them is already yours. **(b) Pin the COUNT, not the presence.** "A panel exists" is true in the broken state too; "exactly one handler is registered" is the assertion that makes the regression impossible to reintroduce quietly. ⚠ Generalises past this control: any per-instance collaborator reachable both by construction and by an installer (renderers, input handlers, margins, adorners) can be doubled the same way, and the symptom is always "the feature works from one entry point and not another" rather than an error. *(Keyboard Manager — etap 1 audit + etap 2 fix, 2026-07-28; `EditorSearch.Install`, `ConnectionExpandBindingProbe.EditorSearch_InstallsOnePanel_AndEditorScopePredicateHolds`)*

284. **A derived value that is typed by hand goes STALE SILENTLY — and a guard against it must key on the value's SOURCE KIND, not on the value, because the correct and the stale versions look identical.** EmberTern showed keyboard shortcuts by writing them into ~25 `UiStrings` constants (`"Continue · F5"`, `"Format SQL · Alt+F"`). When the Keyboard Manager sprint re-bound Format SQL from `Alt+F` to `Ctrl+K`, `ToolbarFormatSqlTooltip` went on reading **`"Format SQL · Alt+F"`** for a whole etap: build 0/0, full suite green, nothing to notice — a tooltip confidently teaching the user a shortcut that no longer existed. That is the characteristic failure of a hand-copied derived fact: it does not break, it *lies*, and the lie is invisible precisely where the original is most trusted. The fix is the ordinary one (one owner — the command catalog — and a composer every consumer reads through). **The interesting part is the guard.** The obvious test is "no user-visible string contains gesture-shaped text", and it cannot work: a *correctly composed* string legitimately contains `" · F7"` at run time, so the runtime value carries no evidence about where the gesture came from. What separates them is the declaration: **a `const` is a literal by definition, a `static readonly` was computed.** So the enforceable rule is "no `const` in `UiStrings` may contain gesture-shaped text", checked by reflection over `FieldInfo.IsLiteral` + `GetRawConstantValue()`, with an allowlist carrying a reason per entry and a second test that fails when an allowlist entry goes stale (names a member that no longer exists, or one that no longer needs excusing). **Two rules worth carrying.** **(a) When you centralise a duplicated fact, ask what observable difference remains between a correct consumer and a stale one — if the answer is "none at run time", the guard has to look at the code shape (declaration kind, call site, type) rather than the data.** **(b) `const` → `static readonly` is a safe migration for a UI-string class and cheaper than it looks**: `x:Static` resolves both identically, so ~25 strings were centralised without touching a single one of the 15 XAML files that consume them — but check first that none is used in a `const` expression (attribute argument, `case` label, another `const` initialiser), which is the one thing that makes it a compile error. ⚠ And prove the guard: a temporary planted `const string PlantedViolation = "Do something · Ctrl+J"` was added, seen to fail the test *by name*, and reverted. A guard nobody has watched fail is a guard nobody knows works — the same discipline as running a regression test against the pre-fix code (#280). *(Keyboard Manager — etap 4, 2026-07-28; `CommandTip`, `UiStringsShortcutSourceTests`)*

285. **Avalonia gives `MenuItem` TWO different ControlThemes — a menu-BAR one and a context/submenu one — so measuring the wrong one "proves" the framework lacks features it has.** Before restyling EmberTern's 32 context menus, a headless probe dumped the realized template of a `MenuItem` to find out whether FluentTheme provides an icon column and a gesture column. It reported only `PART_LayoutRoot`, a `Panel`, `PART_HeaderPresenter` and `PART_Popup` — no icon slot, no gesture slot — which would have justified building a custom context-menu control. **The probe had put the item inside a `Menu`,** i.e. a menu-*bar* item, and Avalonia templates those differently. Re-measured inside a real `ContextMenu` (`contextMenu.Open(host)`), the same control reports `PART_ToggleIconPresenter`, **`PART_IconPresenter`** (wrapping a `Viewbox`), `PART_HeaderPresenter`, **`PART_InputGestureText`** and `PART_ChevronPath` — everything the design needed, so no control was built. **Two rules.** **(a) When you probe a framework control for a capability, construct it in the EXACT container the production code uses** — parent, theme variant and `ControlTheme` all vary by host, and a control that looks feature-poor is usually a differently-templated sibling. **(b) A negative measurement is the dangerous kind**: "the part isn't there" leads straight to re-implementing the control, while a false positive would merely fail later and loudly. Treat any "the framework can't do X" reading as provisional until the probe mirrors the real host. ⚠ Three companion measurements from the same probe, all worth not re-deriving: **`MenuItem.InputGesture` is display-only** (pressing the gesture with the item realized invokes nothing — safe to set on every item, no double-fire); the **icon column keeps its width when the item has no icon** (header presenter at x=28 either way, so labels align with no extra work); and FluentTheme's item padding is **asymmetric** (`11,4,11,7`) at `FontSize` 14 — the tall, slightly-off rows are values, not structure, and a style fixes them. *(Keyboard Manager — etap 5, 2026-07-28; `Themes/ControlStyles.axaml` context-menu styles)*

286. **`IClassFixture` creates ONE fixture PER TEST CLASS — so a fixture whose whole purpose is "exactly one of these per process" is the wrong xunit primitive, and the second consumer is the one that discovers it.** `HeadlessSessionFixture` exists to own the single `HeadlessUnitTestSession` the suite is allowed to have (gotcha #94/#226: a session owns a UI thread, and AvaloniaEdit builds its caret/editing `KeyBinding`s as STATIC lists tied to whichever thread first constructs a `TextEditor`). Its own doc comment said "the ONE headless session for the whole test process", and for a long time that was true only because exactly one class consumed it. Adding a second headless test class as another `IClassFixture<HeadlessSessionFixture>` silently produced **two** sessions, and the suite began hanging inside the new class — a hang `--blame-hang` named immediately. **The fix is `ICollectionFixture` + `[CollectionDefinition]`**: shared across every class in the collection, and it also serialises those classes, which is exactly what a single UI thread needs. The rule belongs on the fixture itself — *join the collection, never add your own class fixture* — because the next person will otherwise reach for the primitive they already know. ⚠ **A second, more valuable lesson came out of the same incident: when several observations of an intermittent failure all name the same test, check whether the name tracks the test or its POSITION.** Four separate full-suite hangs had all named `ConnectionExpandBindingProbe.CompletionRow_HighlightsMatchedPrefix`, and the obvious reading was that that assertion is broken. Running a partition in which a *different* headless class ran last moved the reported name to that other class's last test — at 5901 of 5902 completed, exactly the same shape. So the failure is "the last headless test in a long run", i.e. session teardown, and four consistent observations had been pointing at a coincidence of ordering. **Corroborating observations are only independent if the thing they share is the cause and not the setup.** *(Keyboard Manager — etap 5, 2026-07-28; `HeadlessCollection`, `HeadlessSessionFixture`)*

287. **An icon looks small or large because of its GEOMETRY, not its control — and equalising the geometry's box is the wrong target, because equal extent is not equal visual mass.** EmberTern's `SvgIcon` ControlTheme is a `Viewbox Stretch="Uniform"` wrapping a **fixed 24×24 `Canvas`**, so the Viewbox scales the *canvas*, never the path's ink: every icon renders at exactly the same 24→16 scale, and no stretching compensates for a geometry that fills less of the box. The useful measure is the **ink box** = the path's extremes ±1 (half of the 2 px stroke). Verbatim Lucide `menu` measures 18×14 against `PanelLeft`'s 20×20 — 9.3 px tall rendered where its neighbour is 13.3 px, a 30 % deficit the user spotted immediately. **The instructive part is what happened next.** Enlarging it to exactly `PanelLeft`'s 20×20 made it look **bigger** than every icon around it: three full-width rules are far denser than a thin rectangle *outline*, so at identical extent the hamburger must read heavier. Two rounds of confidently wrong arithmetic, in opposite directions, both caused by optimising a *measurable proxy* (box equality) in place of the actual goal (equal optical size). It shipped at 18×17 — deliberately smaller than its neighbours — chosen by eye from a sheet rendering the candidates beside the real neighbour geometries at the true 16 px. **Three rules.** **(a) Use the ink box to DIAGNOSE ("this glyph is 30 % shorter, that is why it looks small"), never as the goal.** **(b) A dense glyph needs a smaller box than a sparse one to look the same size.** **(c) When a judgement is genuinely visual, build the side-by-side comparison instead of iterating on the live UI** — it converts a guessing loop into one decision. Pin the result as a RANGE (big enough not to look lost, strictly smaller than a closed outline, centred), never as an equality: the equality assertion written in round two would have *failed* the version the user actually wanted. *(Hamburger Navigation — etap 2 QA, 2026-07-28; `Icon.Menu`, `Assets/Icons/ICONS.md`)*

288. **Repeated parallel strokes must be spaced by a multiple of 1.5 in the 24-unit icon grid, or one of them renders visibly thicker than its siblings — and no amount of nudging fixes it, because the cause is the render scale.** A hamburger declared at y4/12/20 drew three geometrically identical rules **three different ways**: the 24→16 scale is ×2/3, so a rule at *y* has its top edge at `2(y−1)/3` with a 1.333 px thickness, and the *fractional part* of that edge decides the anti-aliasing. y4 → band [2.000, 3.333] covers one pixel row at 100 % and the next at 33 % (crisp); y12 → [7.333, 8.667] covers **two rows at 67 % each** — softer, and it reads as thicker; y20 → [12.667, 14.000] is crisp again but mirrored, and its round cap lands on yet another phase, which is why its right end looked clipped. **One cause, two symptoms the user reported separately.** Identical rendering requires equal phases, i.e. `2·Δy/3 ∈ ℤ` ⇒ **Δy a multiple of 1.5**. That constraint binds only the *spacing*, leaving the *extent* free — which matters, because #287's optical-size requirement needs that freedom (a three-rule glyph can be 14, 17 or 20 units tall while staying phase-consistent). ⚠ Exact only at the rendered size the ControlTheme defaults to (16 px); a host overriding `Width`/`Height` re-scales the grid and changes the phase, which is inherent to scaling rather than fixable by coordinates. ⚠ The rule was written for long parallel **rules**, where the eye compares thickness along a length — rows of round dots (Lucide `keyboard`) are not that case, and the upstream geometry is kept there. *(Hamburger Navigation — etap 2 QA, 2026-07-28; `Icon.Menu`, `Assets/Icons/ICONS.md`)*

289. **A guard keyed to a value's CURRENT contents cannot catch a copy of an EARLIER one — and a plausible-looking regex can pass while matching nothing at all.** The About window read its version from the assembly, with a test asserting the version's text appeared nowhere under `src/`. It passed, and the status bar still displayed `EmberTern 0.1.0` from a literal in `MainWindow.axaml` — stale for who knows how long, discovered only because the user saw the status bar and About disagree **on screen**. The guard searched for *today's* number, so a literal left over from an earlier release sails straight past: such a test can only catch a copy someone makes today. The fix is a second guard on the **shape** of a version in the places one can reach a user (XAML `Text=`/`Content=` attributes, non-comment C# string literals), with reasoned exclusions (spec references like `§9.8.1` via a lookbehind; comment lines, because prose legitimately names the literal that was *removed*). ⚠ **The first attempt at that shape guard used a pattern requiring the quote to sit against the digits, and would have caught nothing**: the offending string was `"EmberTern 0.1.0"`, where it does not. It felt like protection while protecting nothing — the more dangerous of the two failure modes, since a false positive merely fails loudly. **Three rules.** **(a) Ask what an EARLIER value would look like, not just the current one.** **(b) Verify a guard by planting the exact string it is meant to catch** — and check the planting too: replacing `ExcelDataReader` with `ExcelDataReaderXX` in a notices file did not trip a substring check, because the longer name still *contains* the shorter, so a passing test looked like a broken guard when the plant was at fault. **(c) Two guards with different keys (the current value everywhere; the value's shape where it can be displayed) cover what neither does alone.** *(Hamburger Navigation — etaps 3–4, 2026-07-29; `AppInfo`, `AppInfoTests`, `ThirdPartyNoticesTests`)*

290. **A `DataGridTextColumn` derives its sort path from its `Binding`, and a COMPILED binding does not give it one — so with `AvaloniaUseCompiledBindingsByDefault` the column headers are clickable and sort NOTHING unless `SortMemberPath` is set explicitly.** Nothing about this fails at build time or throws at run time; the grid simply does not reorder, which reads as "sorting is broken" and is invisible to every test that does not assert display order. It was caught by a headless test that sorted a column descending and asserted the first row *changed* — the assertion existed only because the design had flagged the grid's sort behaviour as something to measure rather than assume. **Two companion facts, both measured on Avalonia 12, worth not re-deriving.** **(a) `DataGridColumn` exposes no public sort-direction property**, so a test cannot inspect the header's direction glyph — assert the row order and leave the glyph to visual QA rather than pretending to cover it. **(b) `DataGridRow.GetIndex()` is obsolete, and `Index` is the index in the UNDERLYING items** — with recycled rows it says nothing about display order; ask the grid what it is showing by setting `SelectedIndex` and reading `SelectedItem`. ⚠ And a third lesson from the same window: an affordance whose visibility was driven by the grid's `Sorting` event **re-armed itself**, because clearing a column's sort raises that same event, late enough that a "we are resetting" guard flag does not cover it. Rather than chase the event's timing the affordance was made **stateless and always visible** — a small button that cannot lie about the grid's state. That is #240's lesson again: never tie a control's visibility to the state its own action changes. *(Hamburger Navigation — etap 5, 2026-07-29; `KeyboardShortcutsWindow`)*

291. **An assertion that a short string is ABSENT from an encrypted or encoded blob is flaky by construction — the alphabet, not the secret, decides whether it passes.** Proving that a settings export really is encrypted needs a test saying "the plaintext does not appear in the file". Asserting it for the connection *name* `"Lab"` passed in isolation and failed in the batch: `L`, `a` and `b` are all Base64 characters, so a three-character sequence turns up in ciphertext by chance every few dozen runs. Nothing was wrong with the production code — the test was measuring randomness. **Two rules.** **(a) When asserting absence from an encoded blob, choose a needle that the encoding CANNOT produce**, and say so where it is chosen: the password fixture is `s3cr3t-database-password`, whose `-` is outside the standard Base64 alphabet, so the assertion can never pass by coincidence and can never fail by it either. **(b) For the structural half, assert the same way**: `{` is likewise not a Base64 character, so *"the payload contains no `{`"* proves it is not JSON — a positive statement about the format rather than a lucky search for one value. ⚠ Generalises past crypto to any test asserting absence from Base64, hex, a compressed stream or a hash: a short needle over a small alphabet is a probabilistic test wearing a deterministic test's clothes, and it will fail once, in someone else's session, on a change that had nothing to do with it. *(Settings Center — etap 5a, 2026-07-30; `SettingsExportContentTests.TheWrittenFileNeverShowsAPassword_EvenWhenPasswordsAreIncluded`)*

292. **When a format omits a field by EMPTYING it rather than by leaving it out, a merge that copies the incoming record wholesale DELETES the local value — and the deletion looks like the feature working.** EmberTern's settings export omits connection passwords by writing every profile with `Password = ""` (the property is non-negotiably non-nullable, and an empty password is already what "none stored" means everywhere in the app). The import merges profiles by `Id`, and the obvious merge — replace the local profile with the incoming one — therefore **erases a working credential as a side effect of importing a host name**. Nothing fails: the profile is correct in every visible respect, the password box is simply empty the next time the user connects, days later. **The rule: for every field a format declares as "does not travel", the reader needs an explicit decision about the LOCAL value, and it is a different decision from whether the field travels.** Here the writer's table (`SettingsExporter.BuildContent`, guarded by reflection so a new field cannot be forgotten) answers *does this leave the machine*, and the merge (`SettingsImportApplier.MergeConnections`) answers *and what happens to the value already here* — two questions, two places, both needed for the same field. ⚠ The general shape is wider than credentials: any sync, merge, restore or "apply defaults" path over a record with non-nullable fields has this hole, and the empty-value convention is what hides it, because absence would have been visible in the JSON while `""` is not. Pin it in **both** directions — declining the field keeps the local value, accepting it replaces it — or the guard passes with the merge stubbed out. *(Settings Center — etap 5b, 2026-07-30; verified by planting the violation, which failed three tests by name)*

293. **A "preserve the old file before we change it" step must be a COPY when the operation MERGES, and a rename only when it overwrites — and the two are one word apart in a design document.** `ApplicationSettingsStore.SaveOverUnreadableFile` renames `settings.dat` aside and writes a fresh file over empty ground, so "rename aside, never delete" is right for it. The settings import inherited that sentence from the design and it was wrong on the operation: an import *merges*, so the file it preserves is also the file it reads the current values out of — moving it away takes the merge base with it, and **every section the user did not select comes back as a default** while the operation still reports success. Take the naming pattern and the never-delete principle from the precedent; decide the verb from what your own operation does. ⚠ A second-order consequence worth copying: because the copy happens *before* the write, a write that is then **refused** would leave a rescue file behind for an operation that never happened — and the only ways out of that are a delete branch on a data-safety surface or unexplained clutter in the user's settings folder.
